			</item>
		<item>
		<title>NarrateAI: production-ready LLM quality assurance on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/narrateai-production-ready-llm-quality-assurance-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Felicia Yue Xiong]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 25 Sep 2026 16:15:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d32b748fe9d6f6dd6643926259f6ad08cf697479</guid>

					<description>NarrateAI delivers production-ready LLM quality assurance on Amazon Bedrock. This post details five techniques—adaptive pipeline orchestration, cross-account multi-model failover, real-time streaming evaluation, composite evaluation, and data accuracy verification—that reach about 99% numerical accuracy while streaming responses in real time.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Executives need to make data-driven decisions during live business reviews, where accuracy and speed matter. A conversational agentic AI assistant can meet this need by answering data questions instantly. But the stakes are high: a wrong number or a slow response in front of leadership carries immediate professional consequences, and a capable large language model (LLM) alone can’t guarantee either. In production, that gap shows up as hallucinated metrics, API throttling, validation latency, and subjective language. Closing it requires production-ready quality assurance built into every step, from data retrieval to response delivery. This post details five techniques, implemented on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, that work together to deliver it: adaptive pipeline orchestration, cross-account multi-model failover, real-time streaming evaluation, composite evaluation framework, and data accuracy verification. Each addresses a distinct failure mode while operating as a coordinated system.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post is the second in our NarrateAI series. NarrateAI transforms business intelligence for over 4,000 AWS executive leaders through a two-layer architecture built on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. The architecture comprises an Automated Narrative Generation Layer for batch processing and a Conversational AI Interface Layer for real-time interaction. Our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-aws-smgs-uses-an-ai-powered-conversational-assistant-to-transform-business-management-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;previous post&lt;/a&gt; covered the business challenges, overall architecture, user experience, and enterprise deployment. This one goes deeper into the engineering, showing how the five techniques achieve approximately 99 percent numerical accuracy while streaming responses in real time. It’s written for engineers and architects building LLM applications who are familiar with LLM APIs and streaming responses.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="from-business-vision-to-technical-implementation"&gt;From business vision to technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post focuses exclusively on the advanced quality assurance mechanisms within the real-time layer. Consider an executive asking “Which regions aren’t meeting their targets and why?” The five techniques work together so that the response is numerically accurate and arrives in real time. It also maintains throughput under concurrent global usage and is professionally formatted for direct use in business reviews.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows how these five techniques form a layered dependency chain, where each layer feeds the one below it to produce a validated real-time response from raw queries. Adaptive Pipeline Orchestration routes queries by data volume so that most complete in a single fast pass while complex queries receive full parallel treatment. Cross-Account Multi-Model Failover expands available inference capacity across independent model-account quota spaces, reducing user-visible throttling. Real-Time Streaming Evaluation validates each paragraph the moment it’s produced, overlapping quality checks with generation. The Composite Evaluation Framework runs multiple independent evaluators in parallel against each paragraph. Data Accuracy Verification catches numerical hallucinations through a two-stage cascade. It starts with cheap exact matching and escalates to semantic verification only when needed. The following sections walk through each technique in the order it appears in the pipeline, from data retrieval through generation to quality assurance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 623px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/23/20546-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/23/20546-1.png" alt="Layered dependency chain of the five quality assurance techniques, from query intake to validated real-time response" width="613"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How the five quality assurance techniques layer together to turn raw queries into validated real-time responses&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="adaptive-pipeline-orchestration"&gt;Adaptive pipeline orchestration&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Enterprise knowledge documents contain extensive information and queries vary enormously in how much of it they need. A focused question like “What’s my team’s quarterly attainment?” might draw on a handful of sections (discrete chunks of enterprise knowledge documents retrieved). A comprehensive request like “Give me a full regional performance analysis” requires synthesis across hundreds. Users expect the same response time either way. This heterogeneity requires a routing strategy that handles the common case quickly without sacrificing thoroughness for complex scenarios.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;No single processing strategy works for all queries, because the right approach depends on how much data a query actually requires. Single-pass concatenation is fast and cheap, but it breaks down when aggregated sections exceed the model’s context window (typically 200K tokens), forcing truncation and quality loss. Fixed multi-pass batch processing avoids truncation, but it pays for multiple LLM invocations on every query, including the roughly 90 percent that don’t need them. Our approach routes each query based on its total aggregated section volume ∣D∣ (where D is the set of retrieved document sections): single pass when the data fits, batch processing only when necessary. The result is a three-phase pipeline that classifies each query once and then processes it along the cheapest path that preserves quality.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="three-phase-adaptive-processing"&gt;Three-phase adaptive processing&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline processes every query in up to three phases: Mode-Aware Consolidation packs retrieved sections and picks a route, Bifurcated Analysis executes that route as either a single fast-path call or parallel normal-path batches, and Conditional Consolidation merges the parallel results when needed. The following table summarizes how the two paths move through these phases before we walk through each phase in detail.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fast path (~90% of queries)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Normal path (~10% of queries)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trigger&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Retrieved volume ∣D∣ ≤ θ&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Retrieved volume ∣D∣ &amp;gt; θ&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 1: Mode-Aware Consolidation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;All sections concatenated into a single chunk&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Sections packed into N batches respecting document boundaries&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 2: Bifurcated Analysis&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Single LLM call, streams directly to evaluation&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;N parallel LLM calls, one per batch&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 3: Conditional Consolidation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Skipped entirely&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Merges N analyses, resolves conflicts, streams the final answer&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The first phase, Mode-Aware Consolidation, operates on retrieved document sections. It applies a greedy first-fit packing strategy to fill context windows up to the token limit while preserving section priority order and section boundaries. For smaller volumes (∣D∣≤θ, where θ is an empirically calibrated character-count threshold), all sections are concatenated into a single chunk, and this is called “fast path”. For large volumes (∣D∣&amp;gt;θ), sections are packed into optimal batches that respect document boundaries and this is called “normal path”. Threshold θ is set relative to the model’s context window limit and calibrated to capture 70–90 percent of queries on the fast path (in practice, approximately 90 percent take this path, as shown in the Results section). Because the execution mode is fixed in this first phase, each downstream phase can optimize for its specific path rather than handling both.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The second phase, Bifurcated Analysis, executes the chosen path. The fast path makes a single LLM call with the complete context and streams results directly to the evaluation pipeline. The normal path distributes batches across N parallel LLM invocations (where N is the number of batches produced by Phase 1) that analyze chunks independently to achieve near-linear speedup.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The third phase, Conditional Consolidation, operates exclusively on the normal path by synthesizing N independent analyses into a unified response. The consolidation LLM receives partial analyses with the original question and applies conflict resolution heuristics while streaming the final answer. The fast path bypasses this phase entirely because the final response only uses one analysis. Figure 2 shows the complete flow, with both paths converging on the streaming response.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 570px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-2.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-2.jpeg" alt="Three-phase adaptive pipeline flow with the fast path bypassing the consolidation phase" width="560"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end flow of the three-phase pipeline, showing the fast path bypassing consolidation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In production, this threshold-based routing delivers outsized efficiency. Approximately 90 percent of queries take the fast path (single LLM call, time-to-first-token (TTFT) within a few seconds, total latency typically under 25 seconds). The remaining 10 percent (complex multi-document queries) receive the full parallel batch treatment (approximately 50–75 seconds compared to under 25 seconds on the fast path) commensurate with their complexity. With N=4 typical batches for normal-path queries, the blended cost per query is approximately 1.4 LLM invocations. That is a 72 percent reduction in LLM invocations per query versus always using the multi-pass strategy, while maintaining full quality coverage for complex queries. Teams should calibrate their own threshold to match their workload’s query volume distribution and keep it below the model’s context window limit.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cross-account-multi-model-failover"&gt;Cross-account multi-model failover&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The adaptive pipeline consolidates information efficiently, but a fast pipeline is only useful if the model behind it stays available. During peak review periods, when thousands of users run their analyses simultaneously, a single throttled request erodes confidence in the tool and drives users back to manual spreadsheet analysis. The standard mitigation, exponential backoff with jitter, proved insufficient. Amazon Bedrock assigns independent quotas per model and per account. Treating each model-account pair as its own capacity space multiplies available throughput without provisioning new infrastructure. Rather than waiting for capacity to free up, we set out to discover capacity that was never contended in the first place. This section shows how treating every model-account pair as its own quota space multiplies capacity without provisioning new infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That capacity hides in plain sight. Amazon Bedrock quotas are independent across two axes: each model has its own limits, and each AWS account receives its own quota. A 3-model × 3-account configuration therefore provides nine independent quota spaces. At runtime, the system explores this grid by attempting the highest-ranking model first, with accounts randomized to help prevent hot-spotting. The implementation is a custom &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands&lt;/a&gt; model provider, a drop-in replacement for the standard &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BedrockModel&lt;/a&gt; that adds transparent capacity expansion while maintaining full compatibility with existing agent code. Figure 3 shows the grid and the cascade path a request follows through it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 739px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/23/20546-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/23/20546-2.png" alt="Failover cascade across a 3-model by 3-account grid of independent quota spaces" width="729"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The failover cascade across a 3-model × 3-account configuration, where each cell is an independent quota space and requests degrade model quality only after exhausting the accounts at the current tier&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="three-mechanism-coordination"&gt;Three-mechanism coordination&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Three mechanisms coordinate to make this work. The first is model ranking, which establishes a quality-speed hierarchy. Queries attempt the highest-ranking model first and cascade only when necessary, so users receive the best available model automatically. The second is account-level distribution, which helps prevent hot-spotting through stochastic load balancing. Before each failover attempt, Python’s &lt;code&gt;random.shuffle()&lt;/code&gt; randomizes the AWS role ARN list order by creating a copy and shuffling it in place. Load then distributes uniformly across accounts over time without requiring complex traffic-shaping algorithms or centralized coordination. Each request gets fresh randomization, naturally achieving approximately 1/N traffic distribution where N is the number of configured accounts. This maximizes aggregate Amazon Bedrock API quota utilization across the account pool. The third is detection and fast recovery, which avoids backoff entirely. The &lt;code&gt;ThrottlingDetector&lt;/code&gt; catches &lt;code&gt;ThrottlingException&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ServiceQuotaExceededException&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;TooManyRequestsException&lt;/code&gt;, immediately attempting the next model-account combination. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Security Token Service (STS)&lt;/a&gt; AssumeRole obtains fresh credentials in 100–200ms, which is negligible compared to the multi-second delays of &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/api-retries.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;exponential backoff with jitter&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Over a six-month production deployment across over 4,000 users, the N×M quota exploration (where N is the number of models and M is the number of accounts) absorbed traffic surges and reduced user-visible throttling during peak periods. The degree of improvement scales directly with the number of model-account combinations configured. Load testing using the Locust framework revealed that the system was able to support model requests from over 100 users concurrently for a sustained period without any failed requests. The following snapshot shows the load test details for over 100 user requests for a streaming response from Amazon Bedrock through the application. The key takeaway is that infrastructure did not need to scale. The quota space expanded instead.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-4.png" alt="Load test results for 100+ concurrent users streaming responses from Amazon Bedrock with no failed requests" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Load test results for 100+ concurrent users streaming responses through the application&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="real-time-streaming-evaluation"&gt;Real-time streaming evaluation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The failover architecture now provides an uninterrupted token stream under quota pressure. However, a reliably delivered response that contains hallucinated revenue figures has only made the problem worse. Availability without accuracy is a liability. Our initial approach implemented quality checks as a sequential post-processing step. It would generate the complete response, run the evaluators, then deliver the validated output. While this achieved high accuracy, time-to-first-content (TTFC) exceeded a minute as users waited for the entire response to generate and evaluate before seeing the output. This tradeoff between accuracy and responsiveness led us to a key question: does the entire response need to be complete before validation can begin? This section presents the parallel evaluation architecture that answers that question and achieves near-zero validation overhead by using the &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/cs/0210001" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;producer-consumer concurrency pattern&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="paragraph-independence"&gt;Paragraph independence&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Validating output paragraph N does not require waiting for paragraph N+1 to be generated. This logical independence makes parallel execution possible. It dramatically reduces time-to-first-content (TTFC), which is the elapsed time from request submission until the user sees the first delivered evaluated content.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In a sequential pipeline, users wait for all paragraphs to generate and &lt;em&gt;then&lt;/em&gt; all paragraphs to evaluate before seeing anything. Our parallel approach validates each paragraph as it’s produced, so users see the first content after only one paragraph generates and evaluates, not after the entire response completes. Only the very first paragraph incurs evaluation latency before the user sees content. Every subsequent paragraph is evaluated &lt;em&gt;concurrently with&lt;/em&gt; generation of the next, so evaluation cost is absorbed within the generation window. For each subsequent paragraph, the delivery delay is simply whichever takes longer, either evaluating the current paragraph or generating the next one.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In practice, paragraph generation takes on the order of a few seconds, while deterministic checks (weasel words, emoji) complete in tens of milliseconds, providing a wide stability margin that keeps evaluation effectively invisible to users in the common case, even with occasional invocation of expensive LLM-based evaluation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="producer-consumer-architecture-and-performance-model"&gt;Producer-consumer architecture and performance model&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 747px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-5.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-5.jpeg" alt="Producer-consumer streaming architecture with a thread-safe paragraph queue between token generation and evaluation" width="737"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Producer-consumer streaming architecture coordinating the producer, bounded queue, and consumer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The implementation coordinates three asynchronous components. The producer task receives tokens from the Amazon Bedrock streaming API and accumulates them into paragraph-sized units detected through configurable heuristics (double newlines for markdown boundaries). Complete paragraphs enqueue in a thread-safe bounded buffer with mutual blocking and First-In-First-Out (FIFO) ordering to preserve coherence. A sentinel value signals completion. The consumer task dequeues paragraphs, executes sequential validation checks such as data accuracy verification, and streams approved content as multi-word chunks for smooth perceived delivery.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In practice, this steady-state behavior splits into two distinct regimes depending on which evaluators are triggered for a given paragraph.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Define &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;λ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;g&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;e&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;n&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; as paragraph generation rate and &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;&lt;em&gt;μ&lt;/em&gt;&lt;sub&gt;&lt;em&gt;e&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;v&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;a&lt;/em&gt;&lt;em&gt;l&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/sub&gt;&lt;/span&gt; as evaluation rate. Buffer utilization &lt;span class="math inline"&gt;$\rho = \frac{\lambda_{gen}}{\mu_{eval}}$&lt;/span&gt;​ determines equilibrium behavior. Given the nature of the evaluation methods (whether deterministic or LLM-based), we would expect a bimodal ρ distribution rather than a single average value.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;On the fast path, paragraphs requiring only deterministic checks (Weasel Word, Emoji) complete evaluation in approximately 79ms median, yielding ρ ≈ 0.095, far below 1. In this regime, the buffer stays nearly empty because consumers wait for generation, and evaluation adds zero perceivable latency. On the slow path, paragraphs triggering LLM-based numerical verification take approximately 2,025ms to evaluate, yielding ρ median ≈ 21.5, well above 1. Here, backpressure naturally rate-limits the producer, helping prevent unbounded queue growth while the consumer catches up. No content is lost and delivery resumes once the paragraph clears.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This split is a direct consequence of the composite evaluation framework discussed later in this post. Most prose clears deterministic checks in milliseconds, while paragraphs containing numerical metrics trigger deeper LLM-based verification.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="streaming-evaluation-results"&gt;Streaming evaluation results&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To validate this architecture, we measured performance across 1,000 production queries (approximately 10,439 paragraphs) in an enterprise deployment environment using Anthropic Claude Sonnet on Amazon Bedrock with business intelligence queries averaging over 10 paragraphs per response. Absolute timing values are specific to this environment, but relative improvements and architectural patterns generalize broadly to streaming LLM deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluation Mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Median TTFC&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compared to our approach&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Parallel streaming&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;13.2 seconds&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;–&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;No quality checks (unevaluated streaming)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;11.8 seconds&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;+1.4s overhead&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Sequential post-generation (baseline)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;100.2 seconds&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;7.6× slower&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The parallel architecture achieves an 86.8 percent latency reduction versus sequential evaluation. The 1.4 second gap versus unevaluated streaming reflects only the one-time first-paragraph evaluation cost before the pipeline reaches steady state.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="composite-evaluation-framework"&gt;Composite evaluation framework&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The streaming pipeline delivers paragraphs to an evaluator consumer, but a single monolithic validator addresses only one failure mode. Enterprise quality assurance requires coverage across multiple orthogonal dimensions simultaneously. Responsible AI in this system is therefore two-sided: Amazon Bedrock Guardrails screens every incoming query before generation begins, and the composite evaluators screen every outgoing paragraph before it reaches the user. Language objectivity, formatting standards, and numerical accuracy each represent distinct failure modes that a single evaluator cannot cover. This section presents a composite evaluation framework that coordinates independent evaluators through a shared interface. A formal extensibility constraint keeps the pipeline compatible with the real-time streaming architecture described earlier.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="extensibility"&gt;Extensibility&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The composite framework is designed for ongoing extension. As established in the Real-Time Streaming Evaluation section, each evaluator contributes to the pipeline buffer utilization ρ, the ratio of evaluation latency to generation time. Adding a new evaluator is valid as long as the combined ρ across all evaluators remains below 1, keeping the streaming pipeline stable. In practice, cheap deterministic evaluators running in sub-millisecond time can be added unconditionally. Expensive LLM-based evaluators must trigger a sufficiently small fraction of paragraphs to keep the weighted contribution bounded.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each evaluator implements a standard interface. It accepts paragraph content as input and returns a pass/fail result with detected issues and suggested corrections. This supports autonomous operation and pipeline-agnostic integration. Teams can add domain-specific evaluators such as toxicity detection, compliance policy checking, or currency format validation without modifying the pipeline, provided the ρ admission constraint is satisfied.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="three-reference-evaluators-and-auto-correctors"&gt;Three reference evaluators and auto-correctors&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We instantiate three evaluators targeting orthogonal failure modes in business intelligence responses.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;WeaselWordEvaluator&lt;/code&gt; enforces objective language through regex pattern matching because executive reports require quantitative statements (“16 percent growth”) over subjective characterizations (“strong growth”). &lt;code&gt;EmojiEvaluator&lt;/code&gt; maintains formal presentation by filtering conversational emoji through Unicode detection. &lt;code&gt;DataAccuracyEvaluator&lt;/code&gt; addresses numerical hallucinations, the highest-risk failure mode in business intelligence systems. Fabricated metrics propagating into decision workflows present critical compliance exposure that necessitates two-stage hierarchical verification for comprehensive validation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The evaluators execute in parallel against the same paragraph, so the total evaluation delay equals the latency of the slowest triggered evaluator. A critical violation from an evaluator triggers paragraph rejection. Correctable issues invoke the paired corrector. Universal passage approves streaming to the user.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Auto-correction transforms detection into actionable remediation. The pipeline applies correctors in sequenced order.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;WeaselWordCorrector&lt;/code&gt; removes subjective adjectives while preserving linguistic flow.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;EmojiCorrector&lt;/code&gt; strips Unicode characters.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;DataAccuracyCorrector&lt;/code&gt; annotates fabrication with “LLM Reasoning” labels to maintain transparency.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The sequencing matters. Text modification runs first, then character removal, then annotation, which maintains stable input for each transformation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="composite-evaluation-results"&gt;Composite evaluation results&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In production, the composite framework flags substantially more paragraphs than an individual evaluator operating alone. Crucially, nearly a quarter of flagged paragraphs triggered two or more evaluators simultaneously, meaning a single-evaluator deployment would have missed the co-occurring failure in a second quality dimension entirely. This multi-dimensional coverage is the primary benefit of the composite approach. Teams extending this framework can plug in new evaluators with a single interface implementation and no pipeline changes as long as total evaluation time stays within the paragraph generation window.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="data-accuracy-verification"&gt;Data accuracy verification&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Of the three evaluators introduced in the Composite Evaluation Framework section, &lt;code&gt;DataAccuracyEvaluator&lt;/code&gt; addresses the highest-stakes failure mode. A response that looks grammatically sound and objectively worded can still hallucinate the exact number that an executive will act on. In practice, this means reporting “$441M” when the actual figure is “$414M,” a discrepancy large enough to drive incorrect resource allocation decisions before anyone catches the error. This failure mode requires a dedicated two-stage verification architecture that cascades from exact value matching through semantic verification. This section presents that architecture, which balances detection accuracy against computational cost. The first stage of this verification performs exact matching to filter ungrounded outputs, while the second stage applies contextual verification through progressive validation. It performs string similarity analysis followed by LLM-based semantic verification.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="hierarchical-verification"&gt;Hierarchical verification&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Hallucination detection presents competing objectives. Comprehensive accuracy requires semantic understanding, yet real-time deployment demands sub-second validation. Monolithic LLM verification achieves accuracy but incurs prohibitive latency of 2–3 seconds per paragraph. Pure pattern matching provides speed but sacrifices contextual understanding. For example, it can correctly extract “$414M” yet fail to detect inappropriate semantic placement. The two-stage architecture resolves this through cascading validation with progressive computational investment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The design addresses distinct error modes requiring different detection strategies. The first error mode is value fabrication, which occurs when LLMs generate metrics absent from the source corpus. For example, reporting “$441M” when documents contain only “$414M” or citing “Q3 2024 revenue” when source covers only H1 2024. These errors admit deterministic detection through exact value matching in the first stage. The second error mode is context mismatch, which occurs when LLMs misapply source-present metrics, such as using revenue figures for cost discussions, applying Q1 growth rates to Q3 queries, or conflating metrics across business units. These errors require semantic verification in the second stage. The hierarchical approach applies appropriate computational effort to each error class. It uses cheap exact matching for obvious fabrications and expensive semantic analysis only where necessary.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Here’s how the two-stage cascade works in practice. The first stage, Exact Value Matching, extracts numerical metrics from both the source documents and the LLM response using regex patterns, then checks whether each metric in the response has an exact match in the source set. Metrics without matches are classified as UNVERIFIED, terminating validation for fabrications at minimal cost. The second stage, Progressive Contextual Verification, operates on matched metrics through two sub-stages. Stage 2a applies &lt;code&gt;SequenceMatcher&lt;/code&gt; computing similarity ρ=ratio(Cr, Cs) between response context Cr and source context Cs for w-character windows (w=50). For high similarity, classification proceeds as MATCH, avoiding expensive LLM calls. Stage 2b employs &lt;code&gt;ContextualDataAgent&lt;/code&gt; for low-similarity cases (σ&amp;lt;τ), performing semantic verification to detect subtle context mismatches. This cascading design concentrates computational investment where needed. String similarity handles clear matches in approximately 1.7ms per metric while LLM verification addresses ambiguity in approximately 1,758ms per invocation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 411px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-6.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-20546-6.jpeg" alt="Two-stage data accuracy verification cascade from exact value matching to semantic verification" width="401"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The two-stage data accuracy verification cascade, escalating from exact matching to LLM-based semantic verification&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The computational cost of each stage differs significantly. The first stage takes approximately 0.3ms using regex and set operations. Stage 2a takes approximately 1.7ms for string comparison. Stage 2b takes approximately 1,758ms for LLM inference. Because most metrics pass the first stage (87 percent in production) and only 30 percent of those require the expensive LLM verification in Stage 2b, the effective cost per metric averages approximately 812ms. This represents a 54 percent reduction versus monolithic LLM verification. The reduction comes from routing most metrics through cheap deterministic checks, with only a small portion requiring LLM verification. The degree of cost savings will vary by workload. Teams with higher proportions of numerical content will see more metrics reach Stage 2b, while text-heavy workloads benefit even more from early termination.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="architectural-integration"&gt;Architectural integration&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The five techniques form a layered dependency chain where each layer supports the one above it. The Adaptive Pipeline consolidates variable document volumes into LLM-ready context and routes 90 percent of queries through a single fast pass. This consolidated output reaches the Multi-Model Cross Account Failover layer, which converts hard API quota limits into transparent routing decisions and provides the uninterrupted token stream that the streaming evaluator requires. Without this availability guarantee, the evaluation pipeline must handle both quality failures and generation failures simultaneously, substantially complicating its design. The Streaming Evaluator runs producer-consumer parallelization against this reliable token stream and keeps validation invisible to users in the steady state. The Composite Framework and Data Accuracy Cascade plug into the consumer task, adding three-dimensional quality coverage and numerical hallucination detection without disrupting the latency budget.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At the system level, in production deployment, the coordinated architecture delivers responses that start streaming in approximately 13 seconds and maintain 99.3 percent numerical accuracy, with sustained availability across every request observed in this six-month deployment, without a dedicated validation infrastructure, quota increases, or serialization bottlenecks.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-bridging-ai-research-and-production-reality"&gt;Conclusion: Bridging AI research and production reality&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When executives ask data-driven questions in front of their leadership teams, accuracy and speed are non-negotiable. A wrong metric or a delayed response carries immediate professional consequences. Meeting this bar required solving fundamental engineering challenges that go far beyond model selection and basic system design: hallucination of metrics, API throttling under peak concurrent load, validation latency that blocks real-time delivery, and subjective language that undermines credibility.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The quality assurance mechanism works as a coordinated pipeline. Adaptive Pipeline Orchestration routes 90 percent of queries through a single fast pass, keeping latency low for the common case while preserving full coverage for complex multi-document queries. Cross-Account Multi-Model Failover can substantially reduce the likelihood that generation stalls by expanding available quota across multiple independent model-account spaces, absorbing traffic surges without user-visible throttling. Real-Time Streaming Evaluation validates each paragraph the moment it is produced rather than waiting for the full response, cutting perceived evaluation latency by up to 86.8 percent. The Composite Evaluation Framework runs multiple independent checks in parallel against each paragraph, catching co-occurring failures that a single evaluator would miss. Finally, Data Accuracy Verification applies a two-stage cascade that catches numerical hallucinations through cheap exact matching first and expensive semantic verification only when necessary, achieving approximately 99 percent numerical accuracy at an average cost 54 percent lower than monolithic LLM verification according to testing.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The result is a system that starts streaming validated responses in approximately 13 seconds, remained available across every request observed during peak global load in this six-month deployment, and produces output accurate enough to go directly into an executive business review. For the thousands of leaders who use NarrateAI daily, these techniques are the reason numerical errors have not surfaced in the reviews, answers arrive during the meeting rather than after it ends, the system remained available across time zones throughout the deployment window, and output goes directly into a business review without a rewrite.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To apply these patterns, start by profiling your query volume distribution to determine whether adaptive routing can reduce your LLM costs (Adaptive Pipeline Orchestration). If you are hitting throttling limits during peak load, cross-account failover (Cross-Account Multi-Model Failover) delivers immediate capacity gains with no infrastructure changes. For teams already streaming LLM output, adding producer-consumer evaluation (Real-Time Streaming Evaluation) is the highest-impact quality improvement. For responsible AI, adopt both halves of the control pair described earlier: Amazon Bedrock Guardrails on incoming queries and grounding checks on outgoing responses. Input screening alone leaves hallucinated content unfiltered, and output grounding alone leaves the system open to prompt attacks and out-of-scope requests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;These patterns generalize beyond NarrateAI to other production LLM systems where accuracy and reliability are non-negotiable. To start building, explore the following resources.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt; — Foundation model (FM) access and configuration.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; — Model availability by AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; — Agent orchestration.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt; — Publicly available framework used in this implementation.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Deploying real-time personalized speech with Qwen3-TTS on Amazon SageMaker AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploying-real-time-personalized-speech-with-qwen3-tts-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Suneesh T]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 25 Sep 2026 16:09:46 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3b00dd9f3c6b3b5dda58a9808f9dba8711724685</guid>

					<description>Deploy the publicly available Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base text-to-speech model from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to a fully managed, real-time endpoint, and clone a voice from a short reference clip. Cross-lingual cloning preserves the speaker's identity across languages.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;With voice cloning, you can generate new speech in a target speaker’s voice from a short reference recording, without retraining a model. You can now deploy the publicly available &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base&lt;/a&gt; text-to-speech model from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; to a fully managed, real-time inference endpoint.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Voice cloning reproduces the vocal identity of a specific speaker. Start with a short recording of the speaker and its transcript. Then supply the new text to synthesize. The model speaks that text in the reference speaker’s voice, without retraining. Media teams, educators, and application developers can use this capability to create personalized voice experiences and localize multilingual content. They can also support accessible communication and preserve a speaker’s identity across languages.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With a self-hosted, publicly available voice cloning model, you control cost and keep audio data within your AWS environment. You can also adapt the model to your domain. With &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;, you can run the model on a fully managed real-time endpoint and handle infrastructure provisioning, health monitoring, and automatic scaling. You don’t manage the underlying GPU servers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to deploy Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart using the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/sagemaker-python-sdk" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt;, and how to invoke the resulting endpoint to clone a voice from a reference clip. It also covers the configuration settings that make this deployment work in practice, along with the Amazon CloudWatch metrics you can use to monitor and right-size the endpoint.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-is-qwen3-tts"&gt;What is Qwen3-TTS&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Qwen3-TTS is a publicly available text-to-speech model family developed by the Qwen team at Alibaba Cloud. It covers 10 languages: Chinese, English, Japanese, Korean, German, French, Russian, Portuguese, Spanish, and Italian. The models use the Qwen3-TTS-Tokenizer-12Hz speech tokenizer and support streaming generation for low-latency, interactive scenarios.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post uses the Base variant, &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base&lt;/a&gt;. It performs voice cloning from only a few seconds of user audio. It can also serve as a base for fine-tuning. For voice cloning, the model takes a reference audio clip and its transcript. It captures the speaker’s vocal characteristics, such as timbre, pitch, and cadence, and applies them to new text. This differs from the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-CustomVoice" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CustomVoice&lt;/a&gt; variant, which generates speech from a fixed set of predefined speakers rather than a user-supplied reference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The model also supports cross-lingual cloning: You can capture a voice from a reference in one language and generate speech in another while preserving the speaker’s vocal identity.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base is available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart alongside Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-CustomVoice and Qwen3-ASR-1.7B. With these JumpStart options, you can use the streamlined deployment shown in this post.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="voice-cloning"&gt;Voice cloning&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With voice cloning, applications can reproduce the vocal identity of a chosen speaker from a reference recording, rather than being limited to a fixed set of predefined voices. This supports a range of use cases across media, customer engagement, education, and conversational AI.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="benefits"&gt;Benefits&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Key benefits of this deployment approach include:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Personalization at scale:&lt;/strong&gt; You can produce speech in a target voice from a short sample, without collecting large training datasets.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multilingual reach:&lt;/strong&gt; You can use a voice captured in one language to generate speech in another while preserving identity.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Deployments align cost with compute usage instead of per-character API pricing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data control:&lt;/strong&gt; Data stays within your AWS account and the Amazon SageMaker endpoint you manage.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="application-use-cases"&gt;Application use cases&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Common applications for voice cloning include:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Content localization:&lt;/strong&gt; You can translate content into multiple languages while preserving the original speaker’s voice.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer experience:&lt;/strong&gt; Contact-center and virtual-assistant responses can use a consistent brand voice.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;E-learning and audiobooks:&lt;/strong&gt; You can present long-form content in a specific instructor’s or author’s voice.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Creative prototyping:&lt;/strong&gt; You can test dialogue and voiceovers before studio production.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Real-time conversational AI:&lt;/strong&gt; Streaming speech recognition and low-latency synthesis can support interactive voice agents.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This solution deploys Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to a real-time endpoint. JumpStart provides the model artifacts and a pre-built serving container, so you don’t write a custom inference handler. You construct a &lt;code&gt;JumpStartModel&lt;/code&gt; object, call its &lt;code&gt;deploy&lt;/code&gt; method, and invoke the resulting endpoint with the Amazon SageMaker runtime client.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The JumpStart container serves the model to generate 24 kHz audio output from text input. This stage design affects how you size GPU memory, as the following section explains.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the real-time inference architecture for the deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-20646-1.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-20646-1.jpeg" alt="Architecture diagram: a client sends text and base64-encoded reference audio to a SageMaker AI real-time endpoint, which routes the request to the vLLM-Omni container’s talker and code2wav stages and returns synthesized audio" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Real-time voice cloning architecture on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The deployment follows the standard Amazon SageMaker AI real-time inference pattern:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The client sends an HTTP request to the Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint. The request body contains the target text, the base64-encoded reference audio, and the reference audio’s transcript.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI routes the request to the vLLM-Omni serving container running on a GPU instance.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The talker stage generates speech tokens from the text and reference voice. The code2wav stage renders them into a waveform.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The response returns to the client as audio in the requested format (this post uses WAV).&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, confirm that you have the following resources and permissions:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; identity with permissions to create Amazon SageMaker models, endpoint configurations, and endpoints, and to invoke the endpoint.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-updated.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt; environment, a notebook instance, or a local environment with the &lt;a href="https://sagemaker.readthedocs.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt; installed.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Sufficient service quota for a supported GPU instance. This walkthrough uses &lt;code&gt;ml.g6.4xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (1x NVIDIA L4 GPU, 24 GB), which is sufficient for the 1.7B model.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A short reference audio clip of the target speaker (a few seconds is sufficient) and a transcript of the words spoken in it.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-1-deploy-the-model-from-jumpstart"&gt;Step 1: Deploy the model from JumpStart&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Construct a &lt;code&gt;JumpStartModel&lt;/code&gt; with the model ID and deploy it. The GPU memory override is the most important setting for this deployment. The following section explains how to configure it. Before running the following code, replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;sagemaker-execution-role-arn&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with the ARN of the IAM execution role that Amazon SageMaker AI uses to deploy the model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.jumpstart.model import JumpStartModel

model = JumpStartModel(
    model_id="huggingface-ttsvoiceclone-qwen3-tts-12hz-1-7b-base",
    model_version="1.0.1", # pin for reproducibility
    instance_type="ml.g6.4xlarge", # 24 GB L4
    role="&amp;lt;sagemaker-execution-role-arn&amp;gt;",
    env={"SM_VLLM_GPU_MEMORY_UTILIZATION": "0.45"}, # required (see the following section)
)

predictor = model.deploy(
    endpoint_name="qwen3-tts-voice-clone",
    accept_eula=True,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint takes several minutes to reach the &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt; status while the container loads the model onto the GPU. You can monitor the endpoint status in the Amazon SageMaker AI console or by calling the &lt;code&gt;describe_endpoint&lt;/code&gt; API.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-2-size-gpu-memory-correctly"&gt;Step 2: Size GPU memory correctly&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;As described earlier, the model runs as two stages (talker and code2wav) on the same GPU. vLLM reserves GPU memory up front based on &lt;code&gt;gpu_memory_utilization&lt;/code&gt;, the fraction of GPU memory a stage can claim. Both stages share one GPU. To avoid out-of-memory errors during startup, keep their combined reservation within the GPU’s capacity.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Set the value so the two stages together fit comfortably. With one shared setting applied equally to both stages, &lt;code&gt;0.45&lt;/code&gt; works well (0.45 + 0.45 = 0.90, leaving a approximately 10 percent buffer):&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;env={"SM_VLLM_GPU_MEMORY_UTILIZATION": "0.45"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At container startup, each stage logs its memory use to the endpoint’s log group in Amazon CloudWatch Logs. The following values confirm the model fits well within a 24 GB GPU:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it measures&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Talker model weights&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;3.66 GiB&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Model loading took 3.66 GiB memory and 0.81 seconds&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;code2wav model weights&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;0.45 GiB&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Model loading took 0.45 GiB memory and 4.73 seconds&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Talker KV cache reserved&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;6.08 GiB&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Available KV cache memory: 6.08 GiB&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Talker KV cache token budget&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;56,928 tokens&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;GPU KV cache size: 56,928 tokens&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The model weights are small (3.66 GiB for the talker and 0.45 GiB for code2wav). Most of the remaining memory each stage is allowed becomes KV cache, the working memory that holds tokens for in-flight requests. At a utilization of &lt;code&gt;0.45&lt;/code&gt; on a 24 GB GPU (about 22 GiB usable), each stage can use up to roughly 10 GiB. Together, the two stages fit within the GPU with headroom to spare. This is why a 24 GB GPU is well suited to the 1.7B model, and a larger GPU is not required.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-3-test-the-endpoint"&gt;Step 3: Test the endpoint&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the endpoint &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt;, test voice cloning. Two details are specific to this JumpStart container and are essential to get a response:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Route selection:&lt;/strong&gt; An Amazon SageMaker endpoint exposes a single &lt;code&gt;/invocations&lt;/code&gt; path, which this container routes to its completions handler by default. To reach the text-to-speech handler, you must pass the custom attribute &lt;code&gt;route=/v1/audio/speech&lt;/code&gt;. Without it, the request is rejected.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Payload shape:&lt;/strong&gt; Use the OpenAI speech schema with &lt;code&gt;task_type&lt;/code&gt; set to &lt;code&gt;"Base"&lt;/code&gt; for voice cloning, and include the reference clip URI and its transcript.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audio formats.&lt;/strong&gt; Provide the reference clip as a base64-encoded &lt;code&gt;data:audio/wav;base64,...&lt;/code&gt; URI. Converting the reference to 24 kHz mono WAV before encoding is the recommended input form. The endpoint returns audio in the format you request through &lt;code&gt;response_format&lt;/code&gt;. This post uses &lt;code&gt;"wav"&lt;/code&gt;, and the model generates 24 kHz mono audio.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following code converts the reference clip and base64-encodes it:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import base64, io, json, subprocess
import boto3, soundfile as sf, imageio_ffmpeg

ffmpeg = imageio_ffmpeg.get_ffmpeg_exe()
subprocess.run([ffmpeg, "-y", "-i", "reference.m4a",
    "-ar", "24000", "-ac", "1", "reference.wav"], check=True)

data, sr = sf.read("reference.wav", dtype="float32")
buf = io.BytesIO(); sf.write(buf, data, sr, format='WAV'); buf.seek(0)
ref_uri = "data:audio/wav;base64," + base64.b64encode(buf.read()).decode()

REF_TEXT = "Transcript of the words spoken in the reference clip."&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="clone-a-voice-for-a-single-sentence"&gt;Clone a voice for a single sentence&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Invoke the endpoint with the target text in &lt;code&gt;input&lt;/code&gt; and the reference clip in &lt;code&gt;ref_audio&lt;/code&gt;. Note the &lt;code&gt;CustomAttributes&lt;/code&gt; routing argument, which is required to reach the text-to-speech handler:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-runtime")

payload = {
    "model": "/opt/ml/model",
    "input": "This is a demonstration of real-time voice cloning using Qwen TTS on Amazon SageMaker AI.",
    "task_type": "Base",
    "ref_audio": ref_uri,
    "ref_text": REF_TEXT,
    "language": "English",
    "response_format": "wav",
}

resp = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName="qwen3-tts-voice-clone",
    ContentType="application/json",
    CustomAttributes="route=/v1/audio/speech", # required to reach the TTS handler
    Body=json.dumps(payload),
)
open("clone.wav", "wb").write(resp["Body"].read())&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The preceding request is the most basic case: one sentence of text returned as speech in the cloned voice. The following patterns build on it, using the same &lt;code&gt;ref_audio&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ref_text&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;route&lt;/code&gt; custom attribute.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Sample audio for this example (open the files provided with this post):&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reference input:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;reference_original_input.wav&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;audio class="wp-audio-shortcode" id="audio-138871-1" preload="none" style="width: 100%;" controls="controls"&gt;&lt;source type="audio/wav" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-20646/reference_original_input.wav?_=1"&gt;&lt;/audio&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generated output:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;cloned_output.wav&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;audio class="wp-audio-shortcode" id="audio-138871-2" preload="none" style="width: 100%;" controls="controls"&gt;&lt;source type="audio/wav" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-20646/cloned_output.wav?_=2"&gt;&lt;/audio&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A single request works well for one sentence or a short passage. For longer content, such as an article or a multi-line script, split it and send one request per sentence or paragraph so the voice stays consistent throughout.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="generate-speech-in-another-language-cross-lingual-cloning"&gt;Generate speech in another language (cross-lingual cloning)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To produce speech in a different language while keeping the same voice, use the same reference clip and set &lt;code&gt;"language"&lt;/code&gt; to another supported language. The following example uses the English reference to generate Chinese speech, preserving the speaker’s vocal identity across languages.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payload = {
    "model": "/opt/ml/model",
    "input": "你好，这是一个语音克隆的测试。",
    "task_type": "Base",
    "ref_audio": ref_uri, # same English reference
    "ref_text": REF_TEXT,
    "language": "Chinese",
    "response_format": "wav",
}
resp = runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName="qwen3-tts-voice-clone",
    ContentType="application/json",
    CustomAttributes="route=/v1/audio/speech",
    Body=json.dumps(payload),
)
open("clone_chinese.wav", "wb").write(resp["Body"].read())&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generated output:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;cross_lingual_cloning.wav&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;audio class="wp-audio-shortcode" id="audio-138871-3" preload="none" style="width: 100%;" controls="controls"&gt;&lt;source type="audio/wav" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-20646/cross_lingual_cloning.wav?_=3"&gt;&lt;/audio&gt;
&lt;h3 id="choosing-an-instance-and-scaling-for-your-traffic"&gt;Choosing an instance and scaling for your traffic&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Choose an instance in three steps. First, confirm that JumpStart supports it. Then, check your &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/sagemaker.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service quota&lt;/a&gt; and confirm that its GPU memory fits the model. For a 1.7B model, a 24 GB GPU (the g6 family, NVIDIA L4) is well matched and cost effective.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To estimate concurrent request capacity, check the KV cache token budget in the endpoint’s log group in Amazon CloudWatch Logs. The log group path is &lt;code&gt;/aws/sagemaker/Endpoints/&amp;lt;endpoint-name&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;. At container startup, you see a line such as:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;GPU KV cache size: 56,928 tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This value is the total KV cache capacity, measured in tokens. It represents the combined token count that the reserved cache can hold across concurrent requests. Divide this budget by the average request size to estimate concurrent capacity. Because TTS requests are typically short, one instance can accommodate multiple simultaneous requests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For higher traffic, add endpoint instances or select an instance type with more GPU capacity. Amazon SageMaker AI automatic scaling can adjust the instance count based on demand.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="monitoring-the-endpoint-with-amazon-cloudwatch"&gt;Monitoring the endpoint with Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI publishes endpoint metrics to Amazon CloudWatch automatically. They fall into two groups: instance-level hardware metrics and invocation-level request metrics. Use them to right-size the instance, tune concurrency, and set alarms.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="instance-hardware-metrics"&gt;Instance (hardware) metrics&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Namespace &lt;code&gt;/aws/sagemaker/Endpoints&lt;/code&gt;, with dimensions &lt;code&gt;EndpointName&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;VariantName&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPUUtilization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The percentage of GPU compute units used by the instances.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPUMemoryUtilization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The percentage of GPU memory used by the instances.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CPUUtilization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The percentage of CPU units used by the instances.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MemoryUtilization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The percentage of system memory used by the instances.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;DiskUtilization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The percentage of disk space used by the instances.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h3 id="invocation-request-metrics"&gt;Invocation (request) metrics&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Namespace &lt;code&gt;AWS/SageMaker&lt;/code&gt;, same dimensions:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;View &lt;code&gt;Invocations&lt;/code&gt; with the Sum statistic to see the total request count over a period.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invocations&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests sent to the endpoint.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;InvocationsPerInstance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests sent to each instance behind the endpoint.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ConcurrentRequestsPerModel&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests being processed concurrently.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ModelLatency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The time the model takes to respond, as viewed from SageMaker AI, in microseconds.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OverheadLatency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The time added by SageMaker AI overhead, in microseconds.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invocation4XXErrors&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests that returned a 4XX HTTP response code.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invocation5XXErrors&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests that returned a 5XX HTTP response code.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;InvocationModelErrors&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The number of requests that did not result in a valid model response.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the endpoint, endpoint configuration, and model when you are finished:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;predictor.delete_endpoint() # deletes endpoint and endpoint config
predictor.delete_model()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post showed how to deploy the publicly available Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base model to an Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoint using Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. It also showed how to invoke the endpoint to clone a voice from a short reference clip and transcript. With a managed real-time endpoint, you control your data and align cost with the compute you use, without managing the underlying infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, use the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart guide&lt;/a&gt; to open JumpStart in Amazon SageMaker Studio. Search for “Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base” and deploy the model by using the configuration in this post. For model details, see the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-TTS-12Hz-1.7B-Base" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen3-TTS model card&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
