			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Speaker-labeled transcription with WhisperX on SageMaker AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/speaker-labeled-transcription-with-whisperx-on-sagemaker-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ayush Sharma]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 24 Sep 2026 16:20:12 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">89ffa52d3ad1a4f1ba4eb44fab5c1bc6ba6dcc60</guid>

					<description>The AWS WhisperX Deep Learning Container packages Whisper, wav2vec2 forced alignment, and speaker diarization into a GPU-ready image. Learn how to deploy it to Amazon SageMaker AI real-time and asynchronous endpoints for word-level, speaker-labeled transcription, plus the production details that matter: the GPU AMI pin, scaling, and cost controls.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Any team working with spoken audio hits the same wall with generic speech-to-text. Think contact-center calls, all-hands meetings, podcasts, depositions, and broadcast media. These workloads need two things that standard transcription gets wrong. First, timestamps land at the utterance level, off by several seconds. Second, there’s no reliable answer to “who said what.” Those gaps make transcripts hard to search, caption, redact, or analyze at scale. A missing speaker label breaks compliance review, and an imprecise timestamp breaks a caption or a redaction.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;WhisperX closes both gaps. It wraps OpenAI’s Whisper with batched inference, adds wav2vec2 forced alignment for precise per-word timestamps, and adds speaker diarization to label who spoke. These capabilities map directly to real workloads. Contact centers can measure talk time, check script adherence, and run sentiment analysis, while teams turn meetings into searchable notes. Media and e-learning teams generate accurate captions (in SubRip Subtitle (SRT) and Web Video Text Tracks (VTT) format) for large content libraries. Time-sensitive uses get text the moment someone speaks. In regulated fields like healthcare, legal, and finance, speaker-labeled transcripts support audits and legal discovery.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The AWS WhisperX Deep Learning Container (DLC) packages all of this into a GPU-ready image. You deploy it to an Amazon SageMaker AI real-time or asynchronous endpoint without building a custom image. In this post, we show how to deploy both endpoint types and when to choose each. We also cover the production details that matter: the GPU AMI pin, scaling, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) setup, and cost controls. This post is part of a multimodal series showcasing specialized AWS DLCs. The series spans three AWS DLCs across four use cases: (1) vLLM-Omni for text-to-speech, (2) vLLM-Omni for image and video, (3) WhisperX for speech-to-text (this post), and (4) llama.cpp.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-is-whisperx-and-what-workloads-it-unlocks"&gt;What is WhisperX, and what workloads it unlocks&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Whisper is a popular open source automatic speech recognition (ASR) model family from OpenAI that transcribes spoken audio into text accurately across many languages. It focuses on high-quality transcription and produces timestamps at the phrase or segment level. WhisperX is an open source project that builds on Whisper and extends it for production workloads. It adds per-word timestamps, speaker labels, and faster transcription, three capabilities that together turn raw audio into structured, analyzable transcripts.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="why-a-purpose-built-deep-learning-container"&gt;Why a purpose-built Deep Learning Container&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The AWS WhisperX DLC is a maintained, GPU-ready image that already contains Whisper, the alignment models, and the diarization weights, with no Hugging Face token required. It follows the standard Amazon SageMaker AI serving contract, so you deploy it like any other model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Serving contract:&lt;/strong&gt; The container serves on port &lt;code&gt;8080&lt;/code&gt; and exposes &lt;code&gt;POST /invocations&lt;/code&gt; for inference and &lt;code&gt;GET /ping&lt;/code&gt; for health checks.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request format:&lt;/strong&gt; The endpoint expects &lt;code&gt;multipart/form-data&lt;/code&gt;, with the audio as the &lt;code&gt;file&lt;/code&gt; part plus optional string fields such as &lt;code&gt;language&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;diarize&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;response_format&lt;/code&gt;. Amazon SageMaker AI passes the &lt;code&gt;ContentType&lt;/code&gt; header (including the multipart boundary) through to the container unchanged.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Output formats:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;json&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;verbose_json&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;srt&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;vtt&lt;/code&gt;, so the same endpoint feeds analytics pipelines and video editors alike.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="choosing-between-real-time-and-asynchronous-endpoints"&gt;Choosing between real-time and asynchronous endpoints&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI supports both real-time and asynchronous endpoints, so you can serve the same WhisperX DLC through either pattern. The decision usually comes down to clip length and interactivity. For long audio, use the asynchronous endpoint: it’s the recommended path when transcription, alignment, and diarization need more time to run. Reserve the real-time endpoint for short, interactive clips that finish within the Amazon SageMaker AI 60-second response cap.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Real-time endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Asynchronous endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Best for&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Short, interactive clips&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Long audio, high-volume batch&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Latency&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Synchronous, must finish &amp;lt; 60s&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Submit-and-poll. No response cap&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Invocation&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;InvokeEndpoint (inline body)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;InvokeEndpointAsync (S3 reference)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;I/O&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Body in request / response&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Input + output in Amazon S3&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Scaling&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Add instances (one request/container)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Add instances. Can autoscale to zero&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Cost profile&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Bills while endpoint is up&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Scale-to-zero when idle saves cost&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Both patterns share the same container contract. Amazon SageMaker AI forwards each request to the WhisperX DLC on port &lt;code&gt;8080&lt;/code&gt;. The real-time pattern is synchronous, and the asynchronous pattern brokers input and output through Amazon S3. Instance selection (&lt;code&gt;ml.g4dn.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for cost, &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; for headroom) and the required GPU AMI pin apply to both, and you scale throughput by adding instances rather than concurrency. The request/response sequence for each endpoint type is shown in its walkthrough section.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account and an Amazon SageMaker AI execution role that can &lt;code&gt;create_model&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;create_endpoint&lt;/code&gt;, and (for asynchronous inference) read and write S3.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPU service quota&lt;/strong&gt; for your endpoint instance type (for example, &lt;code&gt;ml.g4dn.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;WhisperX DLC image URI&lt;/strong&gt; from Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) (for example, &lt;code&gt;763104351884.dkr.ecr.&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;.amazonaws.com/whisperx:3.8.6-cu128-amzn2023-sagemaker&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For asynchronous inference, an S3 bucket whose name contains “sagemaker.” The default &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerFullAccess&lt;/code&gt; policy grants S3 access only to such buckets.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A complete, runnable JupyterLab notebook that walks through these steps end to end is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/speech-to-text-whisperx-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Samples repository&lt;/a&gt;. You can run it in Amazon SageMaker AI Studio against your own audio.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-real-time-endpoint"&gt;Walkthrough: Real-time endpoint&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The real-time endpoint returns the transcript in the same synchronous call. Use it for short clips that comfortably finish within the 60-second cap. Figure 1 is a sequence diagram that traces a single real-time request end to end, from the client call through in-container transcription to the inline transcript.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-21846-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-21846-1.png" alt="Real-time endpoint request and response sequence between the client, Amazon SageMaker AI runtime, and the WhisperX DLC" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Real-time endpoint synchronous InvokeEndpoint flow, in-container transcription, and inline transcript within the 60-second cap&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reading the sequence end to end: the client assembles a &lt;code&gt;multipart/form-data&lt;/code&gt; body (the audio as the &lt;code&gt;file&lt;/code&gt; part plus fields such as &lt;code&gt;language&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;diarize&lt;/code&gt;, and word-level timestamp granularity) and calls &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;. Amazon SageMaker AI forwards the request to the container on port &lt;code&gt;8080&lt;/code&gt;, passing the &lt;code&gt;ContentType&lt;/code&gt; and its multipart boundary through unchanged. Inside the container, WhisperX runs voice-activity detection and batched Whisper transcription, wav2vec2 forced alignment for per-word timestamps, and speaker diarization to label who is speaking. It then serializes the result. The runtime returns the transcript (segments, words with timestamps, and speaker labels) inline to the client, which must complete within the Amazon SageMaker AI 60-second response cap.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="create-the-model-and-endpoint-config"&gt;Create the model and endpoint config&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Register the WhisperX DLC as a model, where &lt;code&gt;IMAGE_URI&lt;/code&gt; points at the WhisperX DLC in Amazon ECR, then create an endpoint config. On every GPU variant, you must set &lt;code&gt;InferenceAmiVersion&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;al2-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-3-1&lt;/code&gt;. Without this pin, the endpoint fails to start with a zero-log &lt;code&gt;CannotStartContainerError&lt;/code&gt;. Allow a generous startup health-check timeout because the weights load lazily.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;sm = boto3.client("sagemaker")
# The WhisperX Deep Learning Container image (Python 3.12, CUDA 12.8, Amazon Linux 2023).
REGION = "us-west-2"
IMAGE_URI = f"763104351884.dkr.ecr.{REGION}.amazonaws.com/whisperx:3.8.6-cu128-amzn2023-sagemaker"

sm.create_model(
    ModelName=MODEL_NAME,
    PrimaryContainer={"Image": IMAGE_URI},  # WhisperX DLC; large-v2 is the default model
    ExecutionRoleArn=ROLE_ARN,
)

sm.create_endpoint_config(
    EndpointConfigName=ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME,
    ProductionVariants=[{
        "VariantName": "AllTraffic",
        "ModelName": MODEL_NAME,
        "InitialInstanceCount": 1,
        "InstanceType": "ml.g4dn.xlarge",
        "InferenceAmiVersion": "al2-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-3-1",  # required for CUDA 12.8 DLC
        "ContainerStartupHealthCheckTimeoutInSeconds": 900,
    }],
)

sm.create_endpoint(EndpointName=ENDPOINT_NAME, EndpointConfigName=ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME)
sm.get_waiter("endpoint_in_service").wait(EndpointName=ENDPOINT_NAME)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="invoke-with-diarization-and-word-timestamps"&gt;Invoke with diarization and word timestamps&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Build a &lt;code&gt;multipart/form-data&lt;/code&gt; body with the audio as the &lt;code&gt;file&lt;/code&gt; part and fields such as &lt;code&gt;language=en&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;diarize=true&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;response_format=verbose_json&lt;/code&gt;. The response contains segments, per-word timestamps, and speaker labels.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def build_multipart(audio_path, fields):
    """Audio as the `file` part + optional string fields, per the WhisperX DLC contract."""
    boundary = uuid.uuid4().hex
    body = b""
    for name, value in fields.items():
        body += (f"--{boundary}\r\n"
                 f'Content-Disposition: form-data; name="{name}"\r\n\r\n'
                 f"{value}\r\n").encode()
    body += (f"--{boundary}\r\n"
             f'Content-Disposition: form-data; name="file"; filename="audio.wav"\r\n'
             f"Content-Type: audio/wav\r\n\r\n").encode()
    body += open(audio_path, "rb").read() + b"\r\n"
    body += f"--{boundary}--\r\n".encode()
    return body, f"multipart/form-data; boundary={boundary}"

body, content_type = build_multipart("audio.wav", {
    "language": "en",
    "response_format": "verbose_json",
    "timestamp_granularities[]": "word",  # word-level timestamps
    "diarize": "true",  # speaker labels
})

resp = sm_runtime.invoke_endpoint(
    EndpointName=ENDPOINT_NAME, ContentType=content_type, Body=body,
)
transcription = json.loads(resp["Body"].read())&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The example GitHub repository uses a public-domain recording of the air traffic control (ATC) communications from US Airways Flight 1549, the 2009 “Miracle on the Hudson” emergency landing. It’s a real multi-party radio exchange with background noise, radio compression, and rapid callsign and frequency readouts. Those conditions make it a strong test of transcription accuracy, word-level timestamps, and speaker diarization. The full approximately 3-minute recording is sent to the asynchronous endpoint, and a 40-second segment to the real-time endpoint.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;audio class="wp-audio-shortcode" id="audio-139973-4" preload="none" style="width: 100%;" controls="controls"&gt;&lt;source type="audio/mpeg" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21846/realtime_endpoint_whisperx-flight1549-demo.mp3?_=4"&gt;&lt;/audio&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Running this against a 40-second segment of the source audio returns the following diarized, word-timed transcript:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;[00:02] SPEAKER_01: Cactus 1549, turn left heading 270.
[00:04] SPEAKER_00: This is Cactus 1539, head first to Placid Crest, I'm hoping it's returning back towards LaGuardia.
[00:11] SPEAKER_01: Okay, you need to return to LaGuardia, turn left heading of 220.
[00:14] SPEAKER_01: 220.
[00:18] SPEAKER_01: Tower, stoppy to park, he's got emergency returning.
[00:22] SPEAKER_01: Who is it?
[00:23] SPEAKER_01: It's 1529, he, uh, bird strike, he lost all engine, he lost the thrust in the engine, so he's returning immediately.
[00:28] SPEAKER_01: Check, it's 1529, which engines?
[00:30] SPEAKER_01: He lost thrust in both engines, he said.
[00:32] SPEAKER_01: Got it.
[00:34] SPEAKER_01: Check, it's 1529, if we can get it for you, do you want to try to land 131.3?&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-asynchronous-endpoint"&gt;Walkthrough: Asynchronous endpoint&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The asynchronous endpoint removes the 60-second cap and is the recommended path for long audio. Input and output are brokered through Amazon S3, and you poll for the result. Figure 2 is a sequence diagram that traces the full asynchronous lifecycle, from the S3 upload and InvokeEndpointAsync call through container processing to the S3 output and failure paths.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-21846-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/22/ML-21846-3.png" alt="Asynchronous endpoint request and response sequence between the client, Amazon S3, Amazon SageMaker AI runtime, and the WhisperX DLC" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Asynchronous endpoint S3 by-reference InvokeEndpointAsync flow, single-worker processing, and results through the S3 output and failure paths&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reading the sequence end to end: the client uploads the multipart body to Amazon S3 and calls &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointAsync&lt;/code&gt; with the object’s &lt;code&gt;InputLocation&lt;/code&gt;. It receives an &lt;code&gt;OutputLocation&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;FailureLocation&lt;/code&gt; immediately rather than waiting for the transcript. Amazon SageMaker AI reads the input from Amazon S3 and forwards it to the container on port &lt;code&gt;8080&lt;/code&gt;. There, the same voice-activity detection, batched transcription, forced-alignment, and diarization pipeline runs, one request per container. On success, the container writes the transcript to the S3 output path, or an error document to the failure path. The client polls the output path for the result and checks the failure path, so a failed job surfaces an error instead of looping indefinitely. Because work is brokered through Amazon S3, this path isn’t bound by the 60-second cap and suits long audio.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="create-the-asynchronous-endpoint-config"&gt;Create the asynchronous endpoint config&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The only structural difference from the real-time endpoint is the endpoint config: it adds an &lt;code&gt;AsyncInferenceConfig&lt;/code&gt; with an S3 &lt;code&gt;OutputPath&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;S3FailurePath&lt;/code&gt;. Set &lt;code&gt;MaxConcurrentInvocationsPerInstance=1&lt;/code&gt; to match the container’s single-worker limit, and keep the same &lt;code&gt;InferenceAmiVersion&lt;/code&gt; pin. &lt;code&gt;IMAGE_URI&lt;/code&gt; is the same WhisperX DLC used for the real-time endpoint.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Same WhisperX DLC image as the real-time endpoint.
IMAGE_URI = f"763104351884.dkr.ecr.{REGION}.amazonaws.com/whisperx:3.8.6-cu128-amzn2023-sagemaker"

sm.create_model(
    ModelName=ASYNC_MODEL_NAME,
    PrimaryContainer={"Image": IMAGE_URI},  # WhisperX DLC
    ExecutionRoleArn=ROLE_ARN,
)

sm.create_endpoint_config(
    EndpointConfigName=ASYNC_ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME,
    ProductionVariants=[{
        "VariantName": "AllTraffic",
        "ModelName": ASYNC_MODEL_NAME,
        "InitialInstanceCount": 1,
        "InstanceType": "ml.g5.2xlarge",
        "InferenceAmiVersion": "al2-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-3-1",
        "ContainerStartupHealthCheckTimeoutInSeconds": 1200,
    }],
    AsyncInferenceConfig={
        "OutputConfig": {
            "S3OutputPath": f"s3://{BUCKET}/whisperx-async/output/",
            "S3FailurePath": f"s3://{BUCKET}/whisperx-async/failure/",
        },
        "ClientConfig": {"MaxConcurrentInvocationsPerInstance": 1},  # 1 request/container
    },
)

sm.create_endpoint(EndpointName=ASYNC_ENDPOINT_NAME, EndpointConfigName=ASYNC_ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME)
sm.get_waiter("endpoint_in_service").wait(EndpointName=ASYNC_ENDPOINT_NAME)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The rest of the workflow is identical to the real-time walkthrough. Building the &lt;code&gt;multipart/form-data&lt;/code&gt; request (the &lt;code&gt;build_multipart&lt;/code&gt; helper and transcription fields) is unchanged. You submit it with &lt;code&gt;invoke_endpoint_async&lt;/code&gt; by S3 reference instead of &lt;code&gt;invoke_endpoint&lt;/code&gt;, then read the transcript from the &lt;code&gt;OutputLocation&lt;/code&gt; and check the &lt;code&gt;FailureLocation&lt;/code&gt;. Cleanup is also the same.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Submitting the full approximately 3-minute recording to the asynchronous endpoint returns the following transcript (first and last five lines shown):&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;audio class="wp-audio-shortcode" id="audio-139973-5" preload="none" style="width: 100%;" controls="controls"&gt;&lt;source type="audio/mpeg" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21846/async_endpoint_flight1549-source.mp3?_=5"&gt;&lt;/audio&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;[00:02] SPEAKER_00: Cactus 1549, turn left heading 270.
[00:04] SPEAKER_01: This is Cactus 1539, head first to Placid Crest, I'm hoping it's returning back towards LaGuardia.
[00:11] SPEAKER_00: Okay, you need to return to LaGuardia, turn left heading of 220.
[00:14] SPEAKER_00: 220.
[00:18] SPEAKER_00: Tower, stoppy to park, he's got emergency returning.
......
[02:38] SPEAKER_02: 2-1-0, 4718, I think he said he's going in the Hudson.
[02:43] SPEAKER_00: Cactus 1529, Houston.
[02:51] SPEAKER_00: Cactus 1529, if you can, you got runway 29 available at Newark, it'll be 2 o'clock in 7 miles.
[03:01] SPEAKER_00: You can fly 4718, climb and maintain 1-2-thousand.
[03:04] SPEAKER_02: 1-2-thousand, and keep it private, please.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A GPU endpoint bills continuously until you delete it. When you are done, delete the &lt;strong&gt;endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;endpoint config&lt;/strong&gt;, and &lt;strong&gt;model&lt;/strong&gt; for both endpoints, and remove any S3 input and output artifacts you no longer need.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# A GPU endpoint bills until deleted. Delete endpoint -&amp;gt; config -&amp;gt; model for each endpoint.
for name, cfg, model in [
    (ENDPOINT_NAME, ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME, MODEL_NAME),
    (ASYNC_ENDPOINT_NAME, ASYNC_ENDPOINT_CONFIG_NAME, ASYNC_MODEL_NAME),
]:
    sm.delete_endpoint(EndpointName=name)
    sm.delete_endpoint_config(EndpointConfigName=cfg)
    sm.delete_model(ModelName=model)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-and-production-considerations"&gt;Best practices and production considerations&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pin the GPU AMI &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Always set &lt;code&gt;InferenceAmiVersion=al2-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-3-1&lt;/code&gt; on GPU variants. The default host AMI ships drivers that fail to start this CUDA 12.8 image.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale by instances, not concurrency &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Inference is serialized to one request per container. Set &lt;code&gt;MaxConcurrentInvocationsPerInstance=1&lt;/code&gt; on asynchronous endpoints and add instances or containers for throughput.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Autoscale asynchronous endpoints to zero &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;For bursty batch workloads, scale the asynchronous endpoint to zero instances when idle to cut cost, and use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sns/latest/dg/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS)&lt;/a&gt; completion notifications instead of tight polling.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Right-size the GPU, and use instance pools for availability &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Use &lt;code&gt;ml.g4dn.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (T4) for cost or &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; (A10G) for headroom. To avoid insufficient-capacity errors, list up to five instance types in an Amazon SageMaker AI instance pool. It provisions the highest-priority type first and falls back automatically when capacity is unavailable.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Secure the S3 artifacts &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Turn on S3 Block Public Access, default SSE-S3 or SSE-KMS encryption, and BucketOwnerEnforced ownership on the asynchronous bucket. Scope the execution role to specific buckets and keys.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Handle personally identifiable information (PII) responsibly &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Transcripts of calls and meetings might contain sensitive data, so encrypt artifacts, restrict access, and apply redaction downstream using the word-level timestamps.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observe and retry &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Monitor with Amazon CloudWatch, alarm on failures, and retry around the cold-start behavior described earlier. For deeper GPU and inference visibility, turn on the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/monitor-and-debug-generative-ai-inference-with-sagemaker-detailed-metrics-and-insights-dashboard-on-cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI detailed metrics and Insights dashboard on CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to deploy the AWS WhisperX Deep Learning Container to Amazon SageMaker AI for word-level, speaker-labeled transcription. We used a real-time endpoint for short interactive clips and an asynchronous endpoint for long, high-volume audio. We covered the production details that matter most: the required GPU AMI pin, single-request-per-container scaling, S3 bucket naming, and cost controls.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To go further, explore the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/whisperx/deployment/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;WhisperX DLC deployment guide&lt;/a&gt;, review &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/async-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI asynchronous inference&lt;/a&gt;, and try the accompanying demo notebook against your own audio. You can also find the full working example in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/speech-to-text-whisperx-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Samples GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="acknowledgements"&gt;Acknowledgements&lt;/h2&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Build a multi-account AI agent with AgentCore Gateway and MCP</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-multi-account-ai-agent-with-agentcore-gateway-and-mcp/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Senthil Kamala Rathinam]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 24 Sep 2026 16:12:47 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">118aa8ce2394e17dd4cd724e7cebe6d8a6cddefb</guid>

					<description>Build a multi-account architecture that keeps each team's data in its own AWS account while giving AI agents a unified way to query across them. A central platform account runs the agent using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway and MCP, while line-of-business accounts expose their data as MCP servers with secure cross-account access and fine-grained authorization.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Enterprises increasingly want AI agents that can reason over data spread across many AWS accounts without copying or centralizing it. Each team keeps its data in its own account for good reasons: clear ownership, scope isolation, and independent deployment lifecycles. But an agent that sees only one account’s data delivers limited value, and connecting it to distributed sources usually means replicating data or untangling cross-account &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt;. The goal is to let data stay where it already lives, in each line-of-business (LOB) account. Only the specific data a request needs flows out at query time, so the underlying datasets do not leave their owning account.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you build a multi-account architecture that keeps each team’s data in its own account while giving agents a unified way to query across them, using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is an agentic service for building, deploying, and operating highly effective agents securely at scale. A central platform account hosts the agent tier and large language model (LLM) inference through Amazon Bedrock. LOB teams expose their data and tools as MCP servers, and the platform account’s AgentCore Gateway gives agents a single endpoint for tool discovery and invocation across registered LOBs. Along the way, you set up cross-account MCP integration, authentication with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and &lt;a href="https://www.okta.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Okta&lt;/a&gt;, fine-grained authorization with Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and the governance controls that support production readiness.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The architecture follows a multi-account model with three layers: a central platform account, distributed LOB accounts, and AgentCore Gateway as the integration layer that connects them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="platform-account-the-agent-control-plane"&gt;Platform account — the agent control plane&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The platform team owns the platform account, which runs the agent on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. AgentCore Runtime is a serverless, framework-agnostic environment with session isolation in dedicated microVMs, consumption-based pricing, and built-in authentication. To keep the walkthrough clear, this post uses a single agent, but the same pattern supports multiple agents in the platform account. The agent connects to the platform account’s Gateway rather than to individual LOB MCP servers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;LLM inference runs in the platform account through Amazon Bedrock. The platform team controls available foundation models (FMs), applies &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;, and tracks costs through a single billing boundary, avoiding the overhead of managing model quotas across dozens of LOB accounts. As demand grows, some organizations distribute inference across several dedicated inference accounts, placing AgentCore Gateway in front as an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inference Gateway&lt;/a&gt; that routes traffic across model providers, selecting the provider based on the request and applying per-team rate limits.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Gateway in the platform account acts as the single MCP endpoint for the agent. It registers each LOB account’s MCP server as a target and, from that one endpoint, provides unified tool discovery with semantic search, centralized authentication through AgentCore Identity, fine-grained authorization with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;observability&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Beyond aggregating MCP servers and acting as an Inference Gateway, AgentCore Gateway supports additional &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-supported-targets.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;target types&lt;/a&gt; that make it a central integration point. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-http.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HTTP targets&lt;/a&gt; bring AgentCore Runtime agents, agent-to-agent (A2A) services, and other HTTP endpoints into the same governed endpoint, each addressable through its own sub-path. The platform team can also apply Amazon Bedrock Guardrails for content safety and configure Policy in AgentCore (&lt;a href="https://www.cedarpolicy.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cedar&lt;/a&gt;) for fine-grained access control, both enforced at the Gateway layer outside the agent’s code.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="lob-accounts-data-and-tools"&gt;LOB accounts — data and tools&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Rather than exposing raw AWS resources (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; buckets, databases, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;) directly, each LOB team packages its data and tools as an MCP server. The retail banking team exposes tools like &lt;code&gt;get_balance&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;get_profile&lt;/code&gt;. The lending team offers &lt;code&gt;get_credit_score&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;search_lending_policies&lt;/code&gt;, where the latter queries the fully managed &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/what-is/retrieval-augmented-generation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)&lt;/a&gt; capability in Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases over bank policy PDFs. This reference architecture wraps a standalone Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base inside the MCP server for fine-grained control over the retrieval pipeline. For new implementations, you can instead attach an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-managed-kb.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt; directly to the Gateway as a native connector, so agents query it with standard MCP calls and you operate no retrieval infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The MCP server runs on AgentCore Runtime in the LOB account, a serverless, framework-agnostic environment with session isolation in dedicated microVMs, consumption-based pricing, built-in authentication through AgentCore Identity, and agent-specific observability. This gives LOB teams full ownership of their tool surface: they decide what to expose and what business logic runs behind each tool, and can change the implementation without affecting the platform agent, as long as the MCP tool interface stays consistent.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="cross-account-integration-gateway-and-identity-connect-the-layers"&gt;Cross-account integration: Gateway and Identity connect the layers&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This architecture follows a hub-and-spoke pattern: each LOB deploys a standalone MCP server (the spoke) using MCP over Streamable HTTP, while AgentCore Gateway (the hub) aggregates them behind a single endpoint. The agent connects to the Gateway as one MCP server, and the Gateway federates tool invocation across registered LOB targets. When the agent invokes a tool, the Gateway retrieves OAuth 2.0 machine-to-machine (M2M) credentials from AgentCore Identity, attaches them to the outbound request, and routes it to the right LOB MCP server, which authenticates the token against Okta’s OpenID Connect (OIDC) endpoint before processing the request locally.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The LOB’s data stays in its own account: the MCP server returns only the specific result the tool produced, not the raw dataset, and that result flows to the platform account as context for inference. The source data isn’t copied or relocated.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/21/ML-20091-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/21/ML-20091-1.png" alt="Multi-account AI agent architecture with AgentCore Gateway, MCP servers, and Cedar policy authorization across platform and LOB accounts." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Multi-account AI agent architecture with AgentCore Gateway and MCP&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following walkthrough traces a user’s question as it crosses account boundaries, invokes distributed tools, and returns a unified answer:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The user logs in through the React webapp, which redirects to Okta for authentication.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Okta validates the user’s credentials and returns a JSON Web Token (JWT) containing identity claims (sub, groups, audience).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The user submits a prompt through the webapp, which reaches &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudFront&lt;/a&gt; over HTTPS.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;CloudFront forwards the request to the FastAPI backend running on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The backend applies Amazon Bedrock Guardrails for personally identifiable information (PII) redaction on the user’s input before it reaches the agent, and again on the agent’s output before it reaches the user.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The backend invokes the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agent&lt;/a&gt; on AgentCore Runtime, forwarding the user’s JWT in the Authorization header for identity propagation.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The agent sends the prompt to Amazon Bedrock for reasoning. Based on the model’s response, the agent determines which tools to invoke.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The agent forwards the user’s JWT to AgentCore Gateway, which uses semantic search for tool discovery across LOB targets. Policy in AgentCore (when a policy engine is associated with the Gateway) evaluates the JWT claims against Cedar rules and permits or denies each tool call by user identity, role, or action. Because the outbound call uses M2M, user-level authorization is enforced here at the Gateway.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For permitted calls, the Gateway retrieves OAuth 2.0 M2M credentials from AgentCore Identity, attaches them to the outbound request, and forwards it to the correct LOB MCP server. Each LOB MCP server validates the inbound OAuth token before processing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The LOB MCP server runs its tool logic: (a) against local &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; tables for structured data lookups, and (b) for the Lending &amp;amp; Wealth LOB, also performs RAG retrieval against Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases over bank policy PDFs stored in Amazon S3 with Amazon OpenSearch Serverless indexing.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Results flow back through the same chain (LOB to Gateway to Agent to backend), with a trace panel in the sample application showing which LOBs were accessed and Policy in AgentCore denials. Each LOB runtime validates the inbound OAuth token. In production, the LOB team configures &lt;code&gt;allowedWorkloadConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; to restrict runtime invocation to requests whose identity chain includes the Gateway, reducing the risk of direct access that bypasses Gateway policy and Cedar authorization.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Strands Agent discovers LOB tools through the Gateway’s &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; method, and queries &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; (Preview) at startup to discover registered LOB MCP servers. Onboarding a new LOB involves adding a Gateway target. The agent discovers the new tools on its next &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; call.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="technical-implementation"&gt;Technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following sections walk through each layer of the architecture: how LOB teams build and deploy MCP servers, how the platform team configures AgentCore Gateway with OAuth outbound authentication and Policy in AgentCore authorization, and how continuous evaluation helps keep the agent reliable as tools and models evolve. For the complete implementation, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-banking-mcp-multi-account" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying repository&lt;/a&gt; and run the deployment script, which bootstraps &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK)&lt;/a&gt; across the four accounts, provisions the platform and LOB resources, deploys the MCP servers and Gateway targets, and launches a React web application on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ECS&lt;/a&gt; behind CloudFront.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The accompanying repository assumes the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS multi-account setup managed through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/organizations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Organizations&lt;/a&gt;, with the platform and LOB accounts in the same organization.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model access&lt;/a&gt; in the platform account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore configured in both the platform account (for the agent, Gateway, and Registry) and each LOB account (for MCP server hosting on Runtime).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An OIDC-compatible identity provider (such as Okta, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt;, or Microsoft Entra ID) with M2M app clients for the OAuth 2.0 client credentials grant. The repository uses Okta.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;LOB data sources (Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, Amazon DynamoDB tables, Amazon S3 buckets, or API endpoints) that the MCP servers will wrap.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="set-up-mcp-servers-in-the-lob-accounts"&gt;Set up MCP servers in the LOB accounts&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each LOB team builds an MCP server with &lt;a href="https://github.com/jlowin/fastmcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;FastMCP&lt;/a&gt; and deploys it to AgentCore Runtime using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore CLI&lt;/a&gt;, exposing the team’s data as structured tools with typed inputs and outputs. Each LOB team configures its server with a &lt;code&gt;customJWTAuthorizer&lt;/code&gt; that authenticates inbound OAuth tokens against Okta’s OIDC discovery endpoint, so requests must present a valid token before they can invoke the LOB’s tools. For production hardening, set &lt;code&gt;allowedWorkloadConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; on the Runtime to the Gateway’s Amazon Resource Name (ARN), which configures it to accept requests only when the identity chain includes that Gateway. This sample relies on OAuth audience validation as its primary access control. Adding &lt;code&gt;allowedWorkloadConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; helps restrict invocations to those arriving through the Gateway, reducing the risk of direct access that bypasses Gateway policy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This snippet shows the Lending &amp;amp; Wealth LOB’s MCP server, combining Amazon DynamoDB lookups with Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases retrieval.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;REGION = os.environ.get("AWS_REGION", "us-east-1")
dynamodb = boto3.resource("dynamodb", region_name=REGION)
bedrock_agent_runtime = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name=REGION)
KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID = os.environ.get("KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID", "")

mcp = FastMCP("lending-wealth", host="0.0.0.0", stateless_http=True)


@mcp.tool()
def get_credit_score(customer_id: str) -&amp;gt; dict:
    """Get credit score and contributing factors for a customer."""
    table = dynamodb.Table("CreditScores")
    resp = table.get_item(Key={"customer_id": customer_id})
    item = resp.get("Item")
    if not item:
        return {"error": f"No credit score found for customer {customer_id}"}
    return item


@mcp.tool()
def search_lending_policies(query: str) -&amp;gt; str:
    """Search the bank's lending policy documents for guidelines,
    eligibility criteria, and regulatory requirements."""
    if not KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID:
        return json.dumps({"error": "KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID not configured"})
    resp = bedrock_agent_runtime.retrieve(
        knowledgeBaseId=KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID,
        retrievalQuery={"text": query},
        retrievalConfiguration={"vectorSearchConfiguration": {"numberOfResults": 5}},
    )
    chunks = []
    for r in resp.get("retrievalResults", []):
        text = r.get("content", {}).get("text", "")
        source = r.get("location", {}).get("s3Location", {}).get("uri", "")
        if text:
            chunks.append({"text": text, "source": os.path.basename(source)})
    return json.dumps({"results": chunks}, default=str)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    mcp.run(transport="streamable-http")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deploy the MCP server to AgentCore Runtime with the AgentCore CLI. The configure step sets the entrypoint and protocol. The deploy step packages and pushes it:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Configure the MCP server
agentcore configure \
    --entrypoint server.py \
    --name lending_wealth_mcp \
    --protocol MCP \
    --disable-memory \
    --non-interactive \
    --authorizer-config '{
      "customJWTAuthorizer": {
        "discoveryUrl": "&amp;lt;OKTA_DISCOVERY_URL&amp;gt;",
        "allowedAudience": ["lobfederation"]
      }
    }'

# Deploy to AgentCore Runtime
agentcore deploy --auto-update-on-conflict \
    --env KNOWLEDGE_BASE_ID=&amp;lt;your-knowledge-base-id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After deployment, the CLI returns a runtime ARN that the platform team uses to register the MCP server as a Gateway target.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="configure-agentcore-gateway"&gt;Configure AgentCore Gateway&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In the platform account, create the Gateway with a Custom JWT authorizer that points to Okta’s OIDC discovery URL and validates the audience (aud) claim to restrict which applications can connect:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;ctrl.update_gateway(
    gatewayIdentifier=gateway_id,
    name="lobfederation-gateway",
    protocolType="MCP",
    protocolConfiguration={
        "mcp": {
            "searchType": "SEMANTIC",
            "supportedVersions": ["2025-03-26"],
        }
    },
    authorizerType="CUSTOM_JWT",
    authorizerConfiguration={
        "customJWTAuthorizer": {
            "discoveryUrl": "https://&amp;lt;your-okta-domain&amp;gt;/oauth2/&amp;lt;auth-server-id&amp;gt;/.well-known/openid-configuration",
            "allowedAudience": ["lobfederation"],
        }
    },
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For outbound authentication to LOB MCP servers, the Gateway uses the OAuth 2.0 client credentials grant (M2M). The platform team registers an OAuth credential provider in AgentCore Identity that stores the Okta M2M client credentials. When the Gateway invokes a LOB MCP server, AgentCore Identity obtains a fresh access token from Okta and passes it in the Authorization header. Register the credential provider and attach it to each Gateway target:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Register an OAuth credential provider (M2M / client_credentials)
resp = ctrl.create_oauth2_credential_provider(
    name="lobfederation-okta-m2m",
    credentialProviderVendor="CustomOauth2",
    oauth2ProviderConfigInput={
        "customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
            "oauthDiscovery": {
                "discoveryUrl": "https://&amp;lt;your-okta-domain&amp;gt;/oauth2/&amp;lt;auth-server-id&amp;gt;/.well-known/openid-configuration"
            },
            "clientId": "&amp;lt;M2M_CLIENT_ID&amp;gt;",
            "clientSecret": "&amp;lt;M2M_CLIENT_SECRET&amp;gt;",
            "clientAuthenticationMethod": "CLIENT_SECRET_BASIC",
        }
    },
)
cred_arn = resp["credentialProviderArn"]

# Create a Gateway target for the LOB MCP server with OAuth outbound auth
ctrl.create_gateway_target(
    gatewayIdentifier=gateway_id,
    name="lending-wealth",
    description="Lending &amp;amp; Wealth --- loans, credit scores, eligibility, policy search",
    targetConfiguration={
        "mcp": {
            "mcpServer": {
                "endpoint": f"https://bedrock-agentcore.{REGION}.amazonaws.com/runtimes/{encoded_runtime_arn}/invocations",
            }
        }
    },
    credentialProviderConfigurations=[
        {
            "credentialProviderType": "OAUTH",
            "credentialProvider": {
                "oauthCredentialProvider": {
                    "providerArn": cred_arn,
                    "scopes": ["lobfederation.invoke"],
                    "grantType": "CLIENT_CREDENTIALS",
                }
            },
        }
    ],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When a LOB tool must enforce per-user access itself (for example, row-level security), AgentCore Identity also provides &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/on-behalf-of-token-exchange.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;on-behalf-of (OBO) token exchange&lt;/a&gt;, where the Gateway exchanges the inbound user token for a downstream-scoped token carrying both the agent’s and the user’s identity. This implementation uses M2M because the Okta developer account used for the sample doesn’t support the OBO flow. AgentCore Gateway also supports the authorization code grant and API keys. For examples, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/mcp/mcp-servers/01-configure-auth" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway outbound authentication samples&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-the-agent"&gt;Deploy the agent&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deploy the Strands agent to AgentCore Runtime with a Custom JWT authorizer for inbound auth. The deployment script applies the authorizer configuration through the AgentCore control-plane API after the initial deploy. At request time, the agent forwards the user’s JWT to the Gateway so Policy in AgentCore can evaluate the user’s claims before routing each tool call:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "agents": [
    {
      "name": "lobfederation-agent",
      "authorizerType": "CUSTOM_JWT",
      "authorizerConfiguration": {
        "customJwtAuthorizer": {
          "discoveryUrl": "https://&amp;lt;your-okta-domain&amp;gt;/oauth2/&amp;lt;auth-server-id&amp;gt;/.well-known/openid-configuration",
          "allowedAudience": ["lobfederation"]
        }
      },
      "requestHeaderAllowlist": ["Authorization"]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After deployed, the agent reads the user’s JWT from the inbound request headers and passes it to AgentCore Gateway. This propagates the end-user identity through to the Cedar policy engine without the agent needing to parse or modify the token:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;@app.entrypoint
def invoke(payload, context=None):
    prompt = payload.get("prompt", "Hello")

    # Read the user's JWT from inbound request headers (passed through by Runtime)
    request_headers = context.request_headers if context else {}
    user_jwt = request_headers.get("Authorization", "")

    # Connect to Gateway with the user's JWT --- Cedar evaluates per-user policies
    mcp_client = MCPClient(
        lambda: streamablehttp_client(
            url=GATEWAY_URL,
            headers={"Authorization": user_jwt},
        )
    )

    with mcp_client:
        tools = mcp_client.list_tools_sync()
        agent = Agent(model=MODEL_ID, system_prompt=SYSTEM_PROMPT, tools=tools)
        result = agent(prompt)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="operate-the-agent"&gt;Operate the agent&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After the agent is deployed, the platform team keeps it reliable through continuous evaluation, safe version rollouts, and observability.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="evaluate-continuously"&gt;Evaluate continuously&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In a multi-account architecture where the agent orchestrates tools across many LOBs, the platform team needs confidence that it keeps performing correctly as tools, models, and prompts evolve. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt; provides a managed framework that helps catch regressions before they reach customers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Online evaluation continuously scores a sample of live production traffic (for example, 10 percent of sessions) using built-in evaluators such as Tool Selection Accuracy, Correctness, and Goal Success Rate. Scores surface in the AgentCore Observability dashboard, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore powered by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;, with alerts when quality drops and no code changes required if the agent already emits &lt;a href="https://opentelemetry.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry&lt;/a&gt; traces. This can surface quiet degradations that latency and error-rate monitoring miss, such as an agent routing lending queries to the wrong LOB.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;On-demand evaluation is a real-time API for development and continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD). The team defines an evaluation dataset once (scenarios paired with expected responses, tool trajectories, and goal assertions), and AgentCore Evaluations replays it on every change using dataset evaluation. Because both modes share the same evaluators, what the team gates on before deployment is exactly what it monitors in production. To close the loop, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/optimization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Optimization&lt;/a&gt; analyzes production traces and recommends prompt and tool-description improvements, validated before they ship.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="version-and-roll-out-safely"&gt;Version and roll out safely&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You deploy the agent on AgentCore Runtime with endpoints (prod, staging, dev) that point to specific versions. When the platform team updates the agent’s prompt or model, it publishes a new version and updates the endpoint, and LOB MCP servers are unaffected because the tool interface doesn’t change. Before promoting a change, the team can run &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/ab-testing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A/B testing&lt;/a&gt; through AgentCore Gateway, splitting live traffic between the current and candidate versions. The platform team then promotes the winning configuration once results reach statistical significance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h4 id="monitor-and-observe"&gt;Monitor and observe&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore provides built-in observability through Amazon CloudWatch and OpenTelemetry. The platform team monitors invocation latency, error rates, and token usage, and views metrics and logs from LOB MCP servers through CloudWatch cross-account observability without switching accounts.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="security-governance-and-cost-management"&gt;Security, governance, and cost management&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Centralizing the agent while distributing data creates specific governance requirements: controlling who can invoke which tools, auditing cross-account calls, enforcing responsible AI policies, and attributing costs back to the LOB that triggered them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="least-privilege-access-and-data-owner-approval"&gt;Least-privilege access and data owner approval&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;LOB teams control who can invoke their MCP server through the JWT authorizer configuration on their AgentCore Runtime deployment. A request from the platform account’s Gateway reaches a LOB’s tools only after the LOB team has configured their MCP server to accept tokens from the platform’s identity provider. This approval is independent of the platform team. Even if the Gateway adds a new target, the LOB MCP server rejects unauthenticated requests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="policy-in-agentcore-authorization"&gt;Policy in AgentCore authorization&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway runs Policy in AgentCore in ENFORCE mode, evaluating rules before routing each tool call against the user’s JWT claims (from the token the agent forwards). Policy in AgentCore uses the Cedar policy language, so rules are explicit permit and forbid statements. For example, a policy can permit read-only tools like &lt;code&gt;get_balance&lt;/code&gt; for all authenticated users while restricting writes like &lt;code&gt;transfer_funds&lt;/code&gt; to a specific role, or block destructive operations like &lt;code&gt;delete_customer&lt;/code&gt; entirely:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-cedar"&gt;// Permit all authenticated users to invoke read-only tools
permit(
    principal is AgentCore::OAuthUser,
    action in [
        AgentCore::Action::"retail-banking___get_customer",
        AgentCore::Action::"retail-banking___get_accounts",
        AgentCore::Action::"retail-banking___get_balance",
        AgentCore::Action::"tools/list",
        AgentCore::Action::"initialize"
    ],
    resource
);

// Block destructive operations regardless of user
forbid(
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"retail-banking___delete_customer",
    resource
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Because Policy in AgentCore uses a default-deny model, only actions with an explicit permit succeed. This gives the platform team centralized control over what the agent can do across registered LOBs, while individual LOB teams retain their own authorization at the MCP server level.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="network-connectivity-and-vpc-considerations"&gt;Network connectivity and VPC considerations&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This reference implementation uses the default public network mode for AgentCore Runtime, where traffic traverses the public internet over HTTPS with OAuth. This suits development but not production. For production, AgentCore Runtime supports virtual private cloud (VPC) connectivity through elastic network interfaces (ENIs) for private resource access, an interface VPC endpoint over &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/privatelink/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS PrivateLink&lt;/a&gt; for private ingress to the Gateway, and &lt;code&gt;allowedWorkloadConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; to restrict runtime invocation to your Gateway. For configuration steps, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/networking-and-content-delivery/network-connectivity-patterns-for-agents-deployed-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-runtime/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;network connectivity patterns for AgentCore Runtime&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-ingress-connectivity-to-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-using-interface-vpc-endpoints/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;secure ingress to AgentCore Gateway using interface VPC endpoints&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="guardrails"&gt;Guardrails&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Apply Amazon Bedrock Guardrails in the platform account for content filtering, PII redaction, and topic restrictions. Because inference is centralized, one guardrail configuration applies to agent interactions across LOB tools. As a newer option, you can apply Guardrails as policies directly on AgentCore Gateway, so checks run at the Gateway layer, outside the agent’s code, covering the tools and context sources routed through the Gateway.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="audit-and-compliance"&gt;Audit and compliance&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To enable data-plane logging, configure log delivery on the Gateway to Amazon CloudWatch Logs, which captures tool invocations and request metadata. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/a&gt; captures control-plane operations (creating and updating gateways, runtimes, targets, and policies) by default. To also capture individual tool calls in CloudTrail, enable data event logging for AgentCore Gateway resources using an advanced event selector. For centralized audit, configure an organization-level CloudTrail trail that aggregates logs from platform and LOB accounts into a dedicated logging account.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="cost-attribution"&gt;Cost attribution&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This architecture provides natural cost boundaries. Each LOB’s data-plane costs (Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, MCP server compute) stay in its own account and appear directly in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/latest/userguide/ce-what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt;. LLM inference and Gateway invocations accrue in the platform account. To attribute these back to the originating LOB, the agent’s execution role carries tags (for example, a lob or costCenter tag). After you activate the cost allocation tag in the AWS Billing console, these tags flow to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/what-is-cur.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Report&lt;/a&gt; for per-LOB chargeback. The agent’s tool trace records which tools each LOB called for proportional allocation. For a deeper approach, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-granular-cost-attribution-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;granular cost attribution for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges after deploying the accompanying repository, run the included cleanup script. It removes deployed resources across the four accounts, including AgentCore components (agent, Gateway, Registry, credential providers), Okta configuration (authorization server, M2M app clients), MCP server deployments, CDK stacks (Amazon DynamoDB tables, Amazon S3 buckets, Amazon Elastic Container Registry repositories, Amazon ECS clusters), and sample data sources.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Run it from the repository root:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./cleanup.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The script runs in reverse order of deployment, removing agent and MCP servers first, then Gateway targets and Policy in AgentCore configuration, then CDK infrastructure stacks across all four accounts.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post showed how to build a multi-account architecture that runs the agent centrally through Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore while keeping data distributed across LOB accounts. LOB teams expose their data as MCP servers, the platform account’s Gateway provides a single authenticated endpoint for tool discovery and invocation, and Policy in AgentCore enforces per-user authorization at the Gateway layer. The pattern scales naturally: to onboard a new line of business, the platform team adds a Gateway target and the agent discovers the new tools on its next invocation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To try this pattern yourself, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-banking-mcp-multi-account" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying repository&lt;/a&gt; and deploy the four-account reference implementation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="related-resources"&gt;Related resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/connect-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-to-cross-account-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Connect Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to cross-account knowledge bases&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/transform-your-mcp-architecture-unite-mcp-servers-through-agentcore-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Transform your MCP architecture: Unite MCP servers through AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Aderant builds intelligent ticket triage with Amazon Nova</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/aderant-builds-intelligent-ticket-triage-with-amazon-nova/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Angela Mapes]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 24 Sep 2026 16:06:46 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Nova]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">427cff8805a421ac08f11c5e6f8349509b2ca46f</guid>

					<description>Learn how Aderant built an intelligent ticket triage system on Amazon Nova Lite through Amazon Bedrock, automating context gathering, classification, routing, and knowledge enrichment for its cloud operations team.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This guest post is co-written by Angela Mapes and Adam Walker of Aderant.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we share how &lt;a href="https://www.aderant.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Aderant&lt;/a&gt;, a global provider of business management software for the legal industry, built an intelligent ticket triage system using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt; Lite through Amazon Bedrock. Aderant’s solution automates much of the context gathering, classification, routing, and knowledge enrichment required for support-ticket triage.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Intelligent Ticket Analyzer supports Aderant’s 38-person SierraOps team, which operates Expert Sierra across 268 client environments globally. It reviews newly submitted, unassigned tickets during scheduled hourly processing cycles on business days. It gathers operational context from Jira, Confluence, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;, and Microsoft SharePoint to recommend a team assignment and resolution starting point, and it automates approved routing and communication actions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;During the first 2.5 weeks of production, from June 30 through July 17, 2026, the analyzer reviewed 109 tickets and achieved approximately 96% routing accuracy. Based on the team’s measured pre-automation triage baseline, Aderant estimates that the system recovers 8–14 engineering hours per week at a total operating cost of less than $30 per month.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The deployment extends &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/aderant-transforms-cloud-operations-with-amazon-quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Aderant’s earlier AI journey&lt;/a&gt; from unified search and on-demand investigation with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; to scheduled, autonomous operational workflows powered by Amazon Nova.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-reduce-operational-toil-without-losing-control"&gt;The challenge: Reduce operational toil without losing control&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Aderant’s SierraOps team processes an average of 34–40 support tickets per week. Before automation, each ticket required 15–25 minutes of manual investigation before resolution work could begin. Engineers had to interpret the request, locate client-environment details, determine ownership, search documentation and ticket history, and then assign or redirect the work.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The cost was broader than triage time alone. Misrouted tickets could remain in the wrong queue until an engineer identified them, while less-experienced team members needed more time or senior-engineer guidance to find the right context and make routing decisions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Aderant needed automated, repeatable investigative workflows so engineers could focus on complex troubleshooting, platform improvement, and proactive operations while preserving human review for lower-confidence decisions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-autonomous-ticket-analysis-with-human-safeguards-using-amazon-nova"&gt;The solution: Autonomous ticket analysis with human safeguards using Amazon Nova&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Aderant built the Intelligent Ticket Analyzer as a serverless workflow orchestrated by a single AWS Lambda function and triggered hourly on weekdays by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt;. For each unassigned ticket in the CloudOps queue, the solution follows five stages:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identify:&lt;/strong&gt; Retrieve unassigned tickets from Jira.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enrich:&lt;/strong&gt; Gather client metadata from Amazon Athena, relevant knowledge from Confluence and SharePoint, and comparable resolved tickets from Jira.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Classify:&lt;/strong&gt; Send the ticket and assembled context to Amazon Nova Lite through the Amazon Bedrock Converse API for structured classification and recommended next steps.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Act:&lt;/strong&gt; Post analysis and acknowledgments, reassign misrouted tickets, notify the appropriate Microsoft Teams channel, and apply predefined actions for support-resolvable scenarios.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observe and improve:&lt;/strong&gt; Publish operational metrics to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; and route lower-confidence classifications to human review.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Routing and reassignment decisions can operate autonomously when confidence meets the configured threshold. Lower-confidence tickets are flagged for human review. The team monitors confidence, misroutes, and processing latency, reviews routing corrections weekly, and uses the findings to refine prompts and routing logic.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="why-amazon-nova"&gt;Why Amazon Nova&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Aderant chose Amazon Nova Lite after evaluating multiple foundation models through Amazon Bedrock using real ticket data. Three factors drove the decision.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational specificity:&lt;/strong&gt; In comparisons using the same ticket content, environment data, runbooks, and resolution history, the team found that Amazon Nova Lite extracted more specific resolution steps and correlated past issues more effectively than the other evaluated models.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native AWS integration:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Nova Lite integrates with the Amazon Bedrock Converse API, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions, the AWS SDK, and the existing AWS environment, reducing the need for an additional external model provider or vendor contract.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Economics at ticket scale:&lt;/strong&gt; The model’s cost profile made it practical to analyze routine tickets, not only high-priority cases. In the initial production period, total system cost remained under $30 per month and Bedrock inference cost remained under $1 per month.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the architecture of the Intelligent Ticket Analyzer. The data flow moves sequentially from left to right through seven stages, all deployed through a single &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/serverless/sam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Serverless Application Model (AWS SAM)&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; template.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21388-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/08/ML-21388-1.png" alt="Architecture data flow from Amazon EventBridge through AWS Lambda to Amazon Bedrock, Amazon DynamoDB, and Amazon CloudWatch" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture of the Intelligent Ticket Analyzer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon EventBridge triggers AWS Lambda. The function retrieves credentials from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;, queries environment metadata through Amazon Athena, and calls Jira, Confluence, and Microsoft Graph APIs for ticket and knowledge context. Amazon Bedrock returns structured classification output from Amazon Nova Lite. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; supports cross-ticket pattern tracking, while Jira, Microsoft Teams, and Confluence receive the resulting actions and updates. Amazon CloudWatch provides operational monitoring.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data boundary:&lt;/strong&gt; The analyzer uses internal operational ticket data, operational metadata, and internal knowledge sources. It does not access, process, or store client matter data or client application business data.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="early-operational-impact"&gt;Early operational impact&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The first 2.5 weeks of production provided an early view of both accuracy and efficiency. Routing accuracy was measured by comparing the analyzer’s assignment with the team that ultimately resolved each ticket. Time savings were based on a four-week pre-deployment baseline of 15–25 minutes of manual triage per ticket.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Early production result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Production measurement period&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;June 30–July 17, 2026&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Tickets analyzed&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;109&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Routing accuracy&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Approximately 96% (4 misroutes)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Average ticket volume&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;34–40 per week&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;8–14 hours per week&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
